Chapter 1 lays out the three categories of the burnt offering, of the herd, of the flocks, and of fowls. Knowing the distinctions of the offerings is not what saves you, but it is your heritage and your history, and you should not be ignorant of it."

              "description": "This session covers Ruth 4 together with 1 Samuel 1-3; these notes cover Ruth 4, the end of the book of Ruth, before the class moves into the history of Israel in first Samuel. The chapter closes the book: Boaz goes up to the gate and conducts the laws of the land before witnesses, the law of inheritance is traced back to Numbers 36 and the law of the husband's brother back to Deuteronomy 25, the nearer kinsman refuses to redeem, Boaz buys the field and takes Ruth to raise up the name of the dead, Obed is born and Naomi becomes nurse unto him, and the generations of Pharez run down to David. Nothing too major happens in Ruth 4 beyond the laws of the land and the law of loving thy neighbour as thyself; that is what Boaz was exercising, and that is why he is mentioned in the scriptures."

Here Israel loses the ark of the covenant in battle, the Most High plagues the Philistines from city to city until they send it back, and the prophet Samuel rises up for the nation of Israel, showing that prayer is a spiritual power: when a righteous man sends up prayer, the Most High will come down in thunder against the enemies of his people. Reading about our forefathers being attacked by the other nations prepares us for the same persecution today; as the works increase, so does the attack, so stay girded up and stay focused."

              "description": "This last chapter records the numbering of Israel. The anger of the Most High was kindled against Israel, so he moved David to number the people, a task that belonged to the Levites and not the king. When David's heart smote him he confessed his sin, and the Most High offered him three judgments through the prophet Gad; David chose to fall into the hand of the Lord, and a pestilence killed seventy thousand men. When the angel stretched out his hand over Jerusalem, the Most High said, It is enough. David then bought the threshing floor of Araunah, refusing to offer that which cost him nothing, built an altar, and the plague was stayed. The thread is that all judgment is in the hand of the Most High, and true worship costs us something."

            {
              "id": "5",
              "type": "Text",
              "title": "1 Chronicles 10: The Death of Saul",
              "description": "Chronicles is written from the standpoint of the southern kingdom, whose registry was kept in Jerusalem. After the name chapters, First Chronicles becomes a recap of First Samuel, retelling the life, troubles, and rise of David. The name chapters are not tedious filler; they were written for a reason. When Israel returned from captivity a registry was kept so that it could be known who was a priest, what tribe a man came from, and where he could be, because each tribe had its own alignment and lane. Read everything and do not skip. This session covers the death of Saul, the anointing of David, his mighty men, the gathering of the tribes, and the moving of the ark. From there the reading moves to Ezra opening the book of the law before all the people at the water gate, the keeping of the Feast of Booths, the Levites recounting the whole history of the Most High's mercy toward a stubborn people, and the sure covenant the princes, Levites, and priests sealed to keep the law. The opening precepts mark up how the Most High deals with a people He gathers, corrects, and restores."

              "title": "Nehemiah 11: Repopulating the Holy City",
              "description": "This session covers the last three chapters of Nehemiah and then opens the book of Esther. Nehemiah 11 and 12 are largely registers of names, the record of who came back and settled Jerusalem, who served as priests and Levites, and how they dedicated the wall with thanksgiving and singing. These are our ancestors, and this is the book of records; a man's name has to be found written in the book to have a part in the inheritance. Nehemiah 13 turns to reform, cutting off the mixed multitude, guarding the Sabbath from buying and selling, and putting away strange wives that had turned the people's hearts and their children's speech away from the law. The thread through it all is order restored according to the covenant given by Moses. Read these chapters as the record of a people applying their laws again once they came back into their land."

            {
              "id": "2",
              "type": "Text",
              "title": "Esther 1: Vashti Refuses the King and the Law of Order in the House",
              "description": "This chapter opens the book of Esther in the days of Ahasuerus, the Persian king who reigned from India even unto Ethiopia over a hundred and twenty seven provinces. It sets the stage for the whole book with a feast, a queen who refuses the king's command, and a decree sent into every province. The thread the teacher draws out is the biblical order of the house: the Most High set the man to bear rule in his own house, and the earth's societies keep reversing that order, putting the woman over the man and even the children over the parents. Vashti's refusal becomes the occasion for a law written among the Persians and Medes that every man should bear rule in his own house. Read this canonical chapter as the record of a kingdom's excess and a decree that, by the providence of the Most High, opens the door for Esther."
            },
            {
              "id": "3",
              "type": "Text",
              "title": "Esther 2: Esther Made Queen and Mordecai Uncovers the Plot",
              "description": "This session covers the book of Esther chapters two through five, picking up after chapter one where Vashti had a spirit on her, thought she could disrespect the king, and lost her royal estate. Chapter two brings in a fair young virgin named Hadassah, also called Esther, the uncle's daughter of Mordecai a Benjamite carried away from Jerusalem in the captivity. She is racially ambiguous, a daughter of Israel who does not look like what people expect, which is why the Most High teaches us not to judge after the sight of the eyes but by whether a spirit bears witness with the Bible. Esther is set as queen instead of Vashti, and Mordecai uncovers a plot against the king. Chapters three through five bring in Haman the Agagite, a descendant of the Amalekites Saul failed to utterly destroy, whose hatred of Mordecai becomes a decree to destroy all the Jews, and Esther is moved to stand for her people for such a time as this."

              "id": "2",
              "type": "Text",
              "title": "Esther 10: Mardocheus Exalted and the Dream Interpreted",
              "description": "This session is short and it crosses a boundary. It opens with the last canonical verses of Esther, chapter 10 verses 1 to 3, where Mardocheus (Mordecai) is set as second unto king Ahasuerus, great among the Jews and accepted of the multitude of his brethren. From there the teaching moves into the KJV Apocrypha, the Rest of the Book of Esther, which fills in what the canonical account left unspoken. Chapter 10 continues into the interpretation of the dream Mardocheus had seen: the little fountain that became a river is Esther, the two dragons are Mardocheus himself and Aman, and the nations gathered were those assembled to destroy the name of the Jews. Chapter 11 gives the dream itself, and chapter 12 records how Mardocheus uncovered the plot of the two eunuchs against the king and how Aman came to hate him for it. The thread the Most High draws out is that these additions do not contradict the canonical book, they line up with it verse for verse, and they show the hand of the Most High saving his people from the enemy who has tried in every generation to wipe them out."

Eliphaz counsels Job that the Most High disappoints the devices of the crafty, saves the poor of His people, and chastens the man He loves. Then Job answers, pleading his case and asking to be taught wherein he has erred. In chapter 7 Job describes his anguish, sleepless nights, and loathsome flesh, wishing for death yet never cursing God. In chapter 8 Bildad the Shuhite argues that the Most High does not pervert judgment, and that Job must have sinned. Throughout, the teacher applies Job's affliction to the last days: the plagues and pandemics Esau creates, the hedge God places around His people, and the call to keep the commandments and endure until the end."

Isaiah 1:1 names Isaiah prophesying in the days of Uzziah, Jotham, Ahaz, and Hezekiah; Hosea 1:1 and Micah 1:1 name the same kings, showing these prophets stood together in one era. We came out of Egypt, then suffered the major captivities of Assyria (Isaiah), Babylon (Jeremiah and Ezekiel), the Persians and Medes (Ezra and Nehemiah), Greece (the Maccabees), and Rome (the New Testament).               "title": "Amos 2: Because They Sold the Righteous for Silver",
              "description": "This session covers Amos 2 through 5, continuing the Most High's judgments that began in chapter 1. The Lord pronounces punishment on Moab and Judah, then turns to Israel for selling the righteous for silver and the poor for a pair of shoes, oppressing the poor, the needy, and the just. The reason we are in captivity even in today's time is the same reason our forefathers went into the Assyrian captivity: they were rebellious against the Most High's laws, would not keep the commandments, and wanted to worship idols. The other nations can do whatever they want, but Israel is punished for their iniquities, and these curses are generational, repeating from captivity to captivity."
